TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF
PROBABILITY MEASURES

arXiv:2510.25315v1 [stat.ME] 29 Oct 2025

LOUIS SHARROCK1 AND CHRISTOPHER NEMETH2
Abstract. We introduce adaptive, tuning-free step size schedules for gradient-based sampling
algorithms obtained as time-discretizations of Wasserstein gradient flows. The result is a suite of
tuning-free sampling algorithms, including tuning-free variants of the unadjusted Langevin algorithm
(ULA), stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD), mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD),
Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD), and variational gradient descent (VGD). More widely, our
approach yields tuning-free algorithms for solving a broad class of stochastic optimization problems
over the space of probability measures. Under mild assumptions (e.g., geodesic convexity and locally
bounded stochastic gradients), we establish strong theoretical guarantees for our approach. In
particular, we recover the convergence rate of optimally tuned versions of these algorithms up to
logarithmic factors, in both nonsmooth and smooth settings. We then benchmark the performance
of our methods against comparable existing approaches. Across a variety of tasks, our algorithms
achieve similar performance to the optimal performance of existing algorithms, with no need to tune
a step size parameter.

1. Introduction
Many tasks in computational statistics and machine learning can be viewed as optimization
problems over a space of probability measures of the form
(1)

π = arg min F(µ),
µ∈P2 (X )

where P2 (X ) is the set of Borel probability measures supported on X ⊆ Rd with finite second moment,
and F : P2 (X ) → R is a functional which maps probability measures to R. Examples include sampling
from a target probability measure (Wibisono, 2018), training mean-field neural networks (Suzuki
et al., 2023b), computing Wasserstein barycenters (Chewi et al., 2020c), distributionally robust
optimization (Kuhn et al., 2019), and marginal maximum likelihood estimation in latent variable
models (Kuntz et al., 2023).
A general strategy for solving problems of this type is to simulate a time discretization of the
gradient flow of the objective functional over the space of probability measures, having equipped
this space with a suitable metric (Ambrosio et al., 2008). Indeed, many well-known sampling
algorithms can be interpreted through this lens. For instance, the unadjusted Langevin algorithm
(ULA), a popular Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) method, corresponds to the forward-flow
discretization of the gradient flow of the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence with respect to the
quadratic Wasserstein metric (Wibisono, 2018; Durmus et al., 2019). Similarly, the mean-field
Langevin dynamics (MFLD), which arises in the analysis of mean-field neural networks, can be
viewed as the forward-flow discretization of the Wasserstein gradient flow (WGF) of an entropy
regularized objective functional (e.g., Suzuki et al., 2023b). Meanwhile, Stein variational gradient
descent (SVGD) can be interpreted as an explicit Euler discretization of the gradient flow of the KL
divergence under a kernelized Wasserstein metric (Liu, 2017; Duncan et al., 2023).
A persistent challenge across all such methods is the selection of a suitable learning rate or step
size parameter. This step size must be small enough to guarantee convergence to the minimizer of
the optimization problem – or a close approximation thereof – while also sufficiently large to ensure
convergence in a reasonable number of iterations. In principle, existing non-asymptotic convergence
guarantees enable one to derive an optimal step size for certain algorithms (e.g., Korba et al., 2020;
Salim et al., 2022; Sun et al., 2023 for SVGD; Dalalyan, 2017a,b; Durmus and Moulines, 2017;
Dalalyan and Karagulyan, 2019; Durmus et al., 2019; Durmus and Moulines, 2019 for ULA). In
practice, however, these optimal step sizes depend on properties of the unknown minimizer (see,
e.g., Theorem 9 in Durmus et al., 2019; Corollary 6 in Korba et al., 2020), and thus provide limited
practical utility.
To illustrate this point, let us consider ULA, a widely used sampling algorithm used to generate
samples approximately distributed according to a target distribution π on Rd , which we assume

Unfortunately, the optimal step size η∗ in (4) cannot be computed in practice, since it relies on several
unknown quantities: the Wasserstein distance W2 (µ 1 , π); the sequence of gradients (∇U (xt ))t∈[T ] ;
2
and the sequence of distributions (µt )t∈[T ] . Thus, in practice, it is not possible to obtain the optimal
upper bound in (5).
In reality, practitioners will often resort to a grid search, running the algorithm of choice (e.g.,
ULA) for multiple step sizes, and selecting the value which minimizes an appropriately chosen metric,
such as the kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD) (Liu et al., 2016; Chwialkowski et al., 2016; Gorham
et al., 2019). This approach, however, is highly inefficient and does not guarantee an optimal choice
of step size. Alternatively, methods based on coin betting ideas from the parameter-free online
learning literature (e.g., Orabona, 2014; Orabona and Pál, 2016; Orabona and Tommasi, 2017) have
shown significant promise, but currently lack rigorous theoretical guarantees (Sharrock and Nemeth,
2023; Sharrock et al., 2023, 2024). Finally, while there have been some efforts to semi-automate the
design of effective step size schedules (Chen et al., 2016; Li et al., 2016; Kim et al., 2022; Coullon
et al., 2023), typically these approaches are still heavily dependent on an appropriate choice of
certain hyperparameters, and have therefore not been widely adopted in practice.
In this context, there remains a pressing need to develop effective and theoretically-grounded
methods for solving optimization problems over the space of probability measures that do not require
manual tuning of the step size parameter. This paper aims to fill this gap.
1In a slight abuse of notation, we use π to denote both the target distribution and its density with respect to the

1.1. Contributions. Our main contributions are summarized below:
• We introduce adaptive, tuning-free step size schedules for optimization algorithms obtained
as time-discretizations of Wasserstein gradient flows (WGFs). In particular, we consider two
time-discretizations of the WGF – the forward Euler discretization and the forward-flow
discretization – and propose adaptive step size schedules tailored specifically to each case (see
Section 4.2.2 and Section A.1.2). Collectively, we refer to our proposed step size schedule(s)
as Fuse (Functional Upper Bound Step Size Estimator).
• We consider, as a special case, the forward-flow discretization of the WGF of the KL
divergence, which corresponds to ULA. In this case, our proposed step size schedule reads

(6)

where rε > 0 is a small positive constant. Thus, the step size is equal to the maximum
Wasserstein-2 distance between µ 1 , the marginal distribution of the algorithm after the first
2
half-step, and (µs− 1 )1≤s≤t , the marginal distributions of the algorithm after each subsequent
2
half-step, divided by the square root of the sum of the squared (L2 (µs ))1≤s≤t norms of the
gradients of the potential.
• We establish rigorous theoretical guarantees for the proposed step size schedules. In particular,
under mild assumptions on the objective functional – e.g., geodesic convexity and locally
bounded (stochastic) gradients – we recover the convergence rate of optimally tuned versions
of the forward-flow discretization (see Sections 4.2 - 4.3) and the forward Euler discretization
(see Sections A.1 - A.2) up to logarithmic factors, in both the smooth and nonsmooth settings.
• Based on our theoretical results, we introduce practical, particle-based, step-size-free algorithms for optimization problems on the Wasserstein space, with a particular focus on the
sampling problem. Our proposed algorithms include tuning-free variants of (parallel-chain)
ULA (see Algorithm 1), in which case our practical step size schedule reads

as well as its stochastic counterpart, stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD) (see
Algorithm 2). We also obtain step-size-free variants of SVGD (see Algorithm 3) and its
mean-field extension, variational gradient descent (VGD); as well as mean-field Langevin
dynamics (MFLD).
• We rigorously benchmark the performance of our methods. Over a wide range of tasks,
our approach achieves comparable or superior performance to the optimal performance of
existing approaches, with no need to tune a step size parameter. In particular, our approach
has a very mild dependence on the initial movement size rε , and thus in practical terms can
be considered entirely tuning-free.
1.2. Related Work. Our work lies at the intersection of two distinct lines of research: parameterfree optimization methods on Euclidean spaces, and optimization over the space of probability
measures. In this section, we provide a detailed survey of both of these areas.
1.2.1. Adaptive and Parameter-Free Optimization. There is a significant body of work devoted
to both the development and analysis of adaptive and parameter-free (stochastic) optimization
algorithms in Euclidean spaces. Below, we review some of the most prominent of these methods.

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

5

Adaptive Methods. Traditionally, the term adaptive refers to algorithms which can adapt (in
the sense of achieving the same or almost the same convergence guarantees) to some property of
the problem at hand (e.g., subgradient bound, smoothness constant), without prior knowledge of
this property. This meaning has somewhat muddied over time (e.g., Orabona, 2025, Section 4.3).
In particular, adaptive is now often used simply to refer to algorithms with (coordinate-wise) step
size schedules which are a function of the past (stochastic) gradients; see, e.g., Section 1 in Li and
Orabona (2019).
There are a number of different (coordinate-wise) adaptive methods. Typically, these methods
can adapt (i.e., do not require knowledge of) the (sub)gradient bound or smoothness constant or
strong convexity constant, but still require knowledge of the (initial) distance to the optimizer (or
the domain diameter).
Such methods include Adagrad – introduced in the online learning community (McMahan and
Streeter, 2010; Duchi et al., 2011) and analyzed in a number of subsequent works (e.g., Li and
Orabona, 2019; Ward et al., 2020; Kavis et al., 2022; Attia and Koren, 2023) – and its variants,
including Adagrad-Norm (Streeter and McMahan, 2010; Faw et al., 2022; Kavis et al., 2022),
RMSProp (Hinton et al., 2012), Adam (Kingma and Ba, 2015; Reddi et al., 2018; Chen et al., 2019),
AdamW (Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019), AdaDelta (Zeiler, 2012), AMSGrad (Reddi et al., 2018; Yang
et al., 2023a), and Shampoo (Gupta et al., 2018), amongst others. For some other related works, see
also Xu and Dai (2012); Levy et al. (2018); Kavis et al. (2019); Luo et al. (2019); Li and Orabona
(2020); Malitsky and Mishchenko (2020); Ene et al. (2021).
Parameter-Free Methods. In this paper, we refer to an algorithm as parameter-free if it does not
require the specification of any tuning parameters. This follows other recent uses of this terminology
(see, e.g. Khaled et al., 2023, Section 2), but differs from more precise definitions originating in the
online learning literature (e.g., Orabona, 2023, Remark 1).
There are a number of traditional parameter-free methods for both deterministic and stochastic
optimization. In the deterministic setting, line search (Beck and Teboulle, 2009; Nesterov, 2015;
Grimmer, 2024) achieves the optimal rate of convergence in smooth and nonsmooth settings with
only an extra logarithmic factor. However, this approach is often expensive and outperformed in
practice (e.g., Malitsky and Mishchenko, 2020).
Meanwhile, Polyak’s step size (Polyak, 1987, Section 5.3) recovers the optimal convergence rate
up to a logarithmic factor for smooth, nonsmooth, and strongly convex optimization (Hazan and
Kakade, 2019), but requires knowledge of the optimum function value. In combination with an
adaptive re-estimation mechanism, this approach also achieves the optimal rate of convergence, up
to a logarithmic factor, when f is Lipschitz (Hazan and Kakade, 2019). The doubling trick can also
be used to render (stochastic) optimization algorithms parameter-free. This approach originates in
the online learning literature (e.g., Cesa-Bianchi et al., 1997; Cesa-Bianchi and Lugosi, 2006; Hazan
and Megiddo, 2007; Shalev-Shwartz et al., 2012; Streeter and McMahan, 2012), and has since also
been studied in the context of stochastic optimization (e.g., Hazan and Kale, 2014).
Parameter-Free Methods in Online Learning. In the online learning literature, parameter-free
algorithms refer specifically to those which adapt to the unknown (initial) distance to the solution,
but may still assume knowledge of other problem parameters (e.g., Orabona, 2023, Remark 1).
There are a number of algorithms which achieve such guarantees, including those based on coin
betting (e.g., Orabona, 2014; Orabona and Pál, 2016; Orabona and Tommasi, 2017; Chen et al.,
2022b) and exponentiated gradients (Streeter and McMahan, 2012; Orabona, 2013). Additional
related works include Chaudhuri et al. (2009); McMahan and Orabona (2014); Orabona (2014);
Cutkosky and Orabona (2018); Jun and Orabona (2019); Orabona and Pál (2021); Orabona (2025).
In the deterministic setting, one can obtain optimization algorithms which do not require knowledge
of any tuning parameters (e.g., the distance to the optimum or the subgradient bound) by combining
parameter-free online learning algorithms (e.g., Streeter and McMahan, 2012; Orabona and Pál,

6

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

2016) with the normalization techniques described in Levy (2017). This is true for both Lipschitz
functions (Orabona and Pál, 2021) and smooth functions (Orabona, 2023).
Recent Parameter-Free Methods. Recently, Carmon and Hinder (2022) proposed a novel
bisection method which recovers the optimal rate of convergence for (stochastic) gradient descent in
nonsmooth convex, smooth convex, and strongly convex settings up to a double-logarithmic factor.
This is better than any bound achievable using parameter-free regret bounds from the online learning
literature, which are translated into stochastic convex optimization bounds via online-to-batch
conversion (e.g., Hazan et al., 2016). However, this method requires resets (i.e., restarting the
optimization procedure multiple times), which can be very expensive in practice, and disregards
potentially useful information gained from previous runs.
Building on this paper, several groups have proposed practical parameter-free optimization
algorithms for both the deterministic (Defazio and Mishchenko, 2023; Khaled et al., 2023; Mishchenko
and Defazio, 2024) and stochastic (Ivgi et al., 2023) settings. Defazio and Mishchenko (2023);
Mishchenko and Defazio (2024) proposed D-Adaptation and Prodigy, which have guarantees for
the deterministic, nonsmooth setting, and achieve highly competitive results on modern machine
learning problems.
Most relevant to our work, Ivgi et al. (2023) introduced Distance over Gradients (DoG), which
recovers the optimal convergence rate of SGD up to a logarithmic factor in high-probability in both
the nonsmooth and smooth settings, assuming the iterates remain stable. A ‘tamed’ version of
their algorithm (T-DoG), whose step sizes are smaller by a logarithmic factor, can achieve a similar
guarantee without an a priori assumption on iterate stability. Inspired by this approach, (Khaled
et al., 2023) proposed Distance over Weighted Gradients (DoWG), a variant of DoG which uses
adaptively weighted gradients. This algorithm matches the performance of gradient descent (in
the deterministic setting) in both smooth and nonsmooth settings, up to an additional logarithmic
factor. There have since been various extensions of these methods (e.g., Dodd et al., 2024; Kreisler
et al., 2024).
1.2.2. Optimization on the Space of Probability Measures. There is also a vast literature
regarding the use of gradient-based methods for solving optimization problems over the space of
probability measures.
Methods. Undoubtedly, the most well-studied and widely used of these methods are Wasserstein
gradient flows (WGFs), which define a notion of gradient flow over the space of twice-integrable
probability measures equipped with the quadratic Wasserstein metric (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008;
Santambrogio, 2017). More precisely, WGFs arise as the solutions to continuity equations driven by
velocity fields that correspond to the direction of steepest descent of the chosen objective functional
(e.g., relative entropy, potential energy) with respect to the Wasserstein geometry. Under mild
conditions, WGFs admit a unique solution (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008, Theorem 11.1.4), and decrease
the value of the objective functional along the trajectory of the gradient flow. Thus, under additional
structural assumptions (e.g., geodesic convexity), they converge to the infimum of the objective
functional (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008, Chapter 11).
WGFs were first identified in the seminal work of Jordan et al. (1998), who showed that the
Fokker-Planck equation describing the time evolution of the marginal law of the overdamped Langevin
diffusion could be viewed as the WGF of KL(·∥π), the KL divergence between the current distribution
and the target distribution π. Building on this, Otto (2001) developed a formal Riemannian calculus
on the Wasserstein space, interpreting probability measures as points on an infinite-dimensional
manifold and introducing a differential structure that enabled the use of tools from Riemannian
geometry to analyze WGFs. This perspective made it possible to describe various diffusion-type
PDEs – such as the porous medium equation – as gradient flows of natural energy functionals. Other
significant early contributions include the work of Villani (2003, 2008) and Ambrosio et al. (2008),
who developed a rigorous theoretical framework for studying WGFs.

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

7

Subsequent work has focused on various aspects of WGFs, including theoretical generalizations;
algorithmic improvements; and practical implementations. One key emphasis has been the development of effective time and space discretizations, which are necessary for the practical implementation
of WGFs. Several different papers study different time-discretizations of WGFs, each leading to
a distinct class of numerical algorithms. These include the backward Euler scheme, which results
in the JKO or proximal point algorithm (Jordan et al., 1998); the forward Euler scheme, which
results in Wasserstein (sub)gradient descent (e.g., Guo et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2022); the proximal
gradient scheme, which results in the Wasserstein proximal gradient algorithm (Salim et al., 2020);
and the forward-flow scheme which, when the objective functional coincides with KL(·∥π), results in
ULA (Wibisono, 2018).
Spatial discretizations are equally essential for practical implementation of WGFs. Existing
approaches include finite-element or finite-volume methods, which discretize the PDE underlying
the WGF (e.g., Carrillo et al., 2015; Benamou et al., 2016; Cances et al., 2020; Li et al., 2020; Fu
et al., 2023). Other popular methods include particle-based methods, which approximate measures
via empirical distributions (e.g., Carrillo et al., 2019). More recently, input convex neural networks
(ICNNs) have been used to parametrize convex potentials in WGFs, offering a scalable, mesh-free
method suitable for simulating high-dimensional flows (e.g., Mokrov et al., 2021; Alvarez-Melis et al.,
2022).
Another line of work considers algorithmic extensions and modifications of the standard WGF. For
example, various authors consider the use of metrics other than the quadratic Wasserstein metric,
leading to a different geometric structure over the space of probability measures, and thus to different
gradient flows. For example, use of a kernelized Wasserstein metric known as the Stein metric leads
to the Stein variational gradient flow (Liu, 2017; Duncan et al., 2023) and, after time-discretization,
the popular SVGD algorithm (Liu and Wang, 2016). Meanwhile, Garbuno-Inigo et al. (2020b,a)
consider the gradient flow induced by the Kalman-Wasserstein metric, resulting in the affine-invariant
Langevin dynamics (ALDI). Other authors have proposed the use of the Fisher-Rao metric, yielding
the so-called birth-death dynamics (e.g., Lu et al., 2023). Finally, Lu et al. (2019b); He et al. (2024)
consider combinations of different metrics; e.g., Wasserstein and Fisher-Rao (Lu et al., 2019b), or
Wasserstein and Stein (He et al., 2024).
Among other extensions of the standard WGF are so-called mirror or mirrored WGFs, inspired by
mirror descent (e.g., Hsieh et al., 2018; Chewi et al., 2020a; Zhang et al., 2020; Ahn and Chewi, 2021;
Li et al., 2022; Sharrock et al., 2023); accelerated WGFs, which are obtained by the introduction of
an additional momentum or velocity variable (e.g., Wang and Li, 2022; Chen et al., 2025; Stein and
Li, 2025); and WGFs on submanifolds of the Wasserstein space, e.g., the Bures-Wasserstein space of
Gaussian distributions (Chewi et al., 2020c; Diao et al., 2023).
Despite many significant methodological developments, there remains a notable lack of work
on tuning-free algorithms for optimization problems on the space of probability measures. While
Euclidean optimization has seen considerable progress in this area (see Section 1.2.1), analogous
developments in the space of probability measures have remained sparse. There have been some recent
efforts to extend parameter-free techniques such as coin betting (e.g., Orabona and Pál, 2016) to the
space of probability measures, with the introduction of so-called coin sampling algorithms (Sharrock
and Nemeth, 2023; Sharrock et al., 2023, 2024). Indeed, these algorithms have demonstrated
comparable performance to that of optimally tuned existing algorithms across a wide range of tasks –
yet they require no manual tuning of a step size or learning rate parameter. Despite their promise,
however, this approach faces significant theoretical challenges. For example, due to the additional
complexity introduced by the geometry of the space of probability measures, existing theoretical tools
used to analyze the convergence properties of coin betting algorithms in Euclidean spaces cannot
easily be applied (see, e.g., Sharrock and Nemeth, 2023, Appendix A). As a result, coin sampling
algorithms still lack rigorous theoretical guarantees (e.g., non-asymptotic convergence rates), even
under strong assumptions on the target measure (e.g., log-concavity).

8

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

Applications. Over the past two decades, WGFs have found application in a wide variety of
problems across computational statistics, machine learning, and applied mathematics. Below, we
survey two prominent examples, which we will later revisit in our numerical experiments.
Example 1 (Sampling from a Target Probability Distribution). The task of sampling
from a probability distribution π, with density π(x) ∝ e−U (x) w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure, is of
fundamental importance to Bayesian inference (Robert and Casella, 2004; Stuart, 2010; Fearnhead
et al., 2025), machine learning (Neal, 1996; Andrieu et al., 2003; Welling and Teh, 2011; Wilson
and Izmailov, 2020), molecular dynamics (Krauth, 2006; Lelièvre and Stoltz, 2016; Leimkuhler and
Matthews, 2016), and scientific computing (MacKay, 2003; Liu, 2009).
It is now well known that the sampling problem can be reformulated as an optimization problem
over the space of measures (e.g., Jordan et al., 1998; Wibisono, 2018; Durmus et al., 2019). In this
setting, one views the target π as the solution of
(8)

π = arg min F(µ),
µ∈P2 (Rd )

moment, and F : P2 (Rd ) → R is a functional which is uniquely minimized at π. Perhaps the most
popular choice of functional in this context is F(µ) = KL(µ∥π), the KL divergence of µ with respect
to π, defined according to

denotes the Radon-Nikodym derivative of µ with respect to π. For this choice of F, it is
straightforward to verify that F(µ) = KL(µ∥π) ≥ 0 for all µ ∈ P2 (Rd ), with F(µ) = KL(µ∥π) = 0 if
and only if µ = π. Thus, in particular, F(µ) = KL(µ∥π) is indeed uniquely minimized at the target
measure π.
As noted earlier, many popular sampling algorithms can be viewed as discretizations of gradient
flows of the KL divergence over the space of probability measures. These include classical MCMC
algorithms such as ULA (e.g., Wibisono, 2018; Durmus et al., 2019), as well as more recent ParVI
algorithms such as SVGD (Liu and Wang, 2016), Laplacian adjusted Wasserstein gradient descent
(LAWGD) (Chewi et al., 2020b), and the various particle-based algorithms described in Chen et al.
(2018); Liu et al. (2019a,b). For different choices of the objective functional, one also obtains various
other sampling algorithms, including the Wasserstein proximal gradient algorithm (Salim et al., 2020),
kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD) descent (Korba et al., 2021), and mollified interaction energy descent
(MIED) (Li et al., 2023).
The connections between sampling and optimization have, in recent years, given rise to a flourishing
literature. For a more comprehensive overview of this topic, we refer to any one of a number of
recent survey papers (e.g., Chen et al., 2023a,b; Trillos et al., 2023; Chewi et al., 2024b; Chewi,
2025; Sharrock and Nemeth, 2025).

Example 2 (Training Neural Networks in the Mean-Field Regime). Over the past
decade, neural networks have achieved remarkable success across a wide range of domains, including
computer vision, natural language processing, and scientific computing (e.g., LeCun et al., 2015).
However, obtaining rigorous mathematical theory to explain their empirical success remains a
significant challenge. In general, learning the parameters of a neural network involves solving a
high-dimensional, highly non-convex optimization problem via stochastic gradient descent, for which
convergence results are very hard to obtain (Danilova et al., 2022).
Recently, significant theoretical progress has been made on this front by considering neural networks
in a suitable scaling limit: the so-called mean-field regime (e.g., Chizat and Bach, 2018; Mei

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

9

et al., 2018; Sirignano and Spiliopoulos, 2020b,a; De Bortoli et al., 2020; Rotskoff and VandenEijnden, 2022). Specifically, consider the task of learning a neural network with M trainable neurons,

1 Pn

where λ ∈ R+ is the regularization strength, and r : Rd → R is a regularization term, e.g., r(θ) = ∥θ∥2 .
In other words, training a mean-field neural network is equivalent to solving the following optimization
problem over the space of probability measures:
(13)

µ⋆ = arg min F(µ),
µ∈P2 (Rd )

where F : P2 (Rd ) → R is as defined in (12). By lifting the task of learning a neural network to
an infinite-dimensional optimization problem on the space of probability measures, it is possible
to leverage the convexity of the loss function to establish the global convergence of gradient-based
optimization methods (e.g., Mei et al., 2018; Chizat and Bach, 2018; Rotskoff and Vanden-Eijnden,
2018; Araújo et al., 2019; Mei et al., 2019; Javanmard et al., 2020; Sirignano and Spiliopoulos,
2020a,b; Rotskoff and Vanden-Eijnden, 2022).
One popular method for solving this optimization problem is the so-called mean-field Langevin
dynamics (MFLD). This can be viewed as the continuous-time limit of a noisy gradient descent
algorithm on the infinite-width neural network, in which the additional noise encourages exploration
and enables global convergence (e.g., Mei et al., 2018; Hu et al., 2021). Alternatively, these dynamics
can be viewed as the WGF of an entropy-regularized version of the objective function in (12) (e.g.
Mei et al., 2018; Hu et al., 2021; Chen et al., 2022a; Chizat, 2022a; Nitanda et al., 2022; Suzuki
et al., 2023b).
Based on this perspective, there has been significant progress towards understanding the theoretical
properties of the MFLD. Mei et al. (2018); Hu et al. (2021); Chizat (2022a); Nitanda et al. (2022);
Chizat (2022a) all show exponential convergence of the MFLD to its unique invariant distribution,
i.e., the minimizer of the entropy-regularized energy functional. Meanwhile, Chen et al. (2022a);
Suzuki et al. (2023a) establish uniform-in-time propagation of chaos, i.e., global upper bounds for the
distance between the empirical distribution of the interacting particle system and the distribution of
the mean-field dynamics. Nitanda et al. (2022) establish the convergence rate of a time-discretization
of the MFLD. Finally, Suzuki et al. (2023b) provide convergence rates for a space-time discretization
of the MFLD. Many of these results have since been extended to the underdamped case, including
exponential convergence to the unique invariant measure (Kazeykina et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2024a),
uniform-in-time propagation of chaos (Chen et al., 2024a; Fu and Wilson, 2024), and convergence
rates for a space-time discretization (Fu and Wilson, 2024).

10

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

There are numerous other examples of optimization problems over the space of probability
measures, which we do not have time to survey in detail here. We mention, amongst others:
generalized and post-Bayesian inference (e.g., Knoblauch et al., 2022; Chazal et al., 2025; McLatchie
et al., 2025), variational inference (VI) including mean-field VI (Yao and Yang, 2022; Lacker, 2023;
Jiang et al., 2024) and Gaussian VI (Lambert et al., 2022; Diao et al., 2023), trajectory inference
in stochastic differential equations (SDEs) (Chizat et al., 2022), multi-objective optimization (Ren
et al., 2024), computing Wasserstein barycenters (Chewi et al., 2020c), non-parametric maximum
likelihood estimation (Yan et al., 2024), mathematical modelling of transformers (Geshkovski et al.,
2025), reinforcement learning and policy optimization (Zhang et al., 2018), distributionally robust
optimization (e.g., Mohajerin Esfahani and Kuhn, 2018; Kuhn et al., 2019; Rahimian and Mehrotra,
2022; Gao and Kleywegt, 2023), generative modelling (e.g., Arbel et al., 2019; Cheng et al., 2024;
Haviv et al., 2024; Galashov et al., 2025), sparse deconvolution (Chizat, 2022b), lossy compression
(e.g., Yang et al., 2023b), and the modelling of granular media (e.g., Otto, 2001). In principle, the
methodology developed in this paper could be applied to any one of these domains.
1.3. Paper Organization. The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we
recall some basic definitions relating to optimization on the Wasserstein space (e.g., the Wasserstein
metric, geodesic convexity, subdifferential calculus in the Wasserstein space). In Section 3, we
introduce standard methods for performing optimization on this space (e.g., WGFs and their timediscretizations). In Section 4, we introduce our dynamic, tuning-free step size schedule, and present
our main theoretical results. In Section 5, we propose practical, particle-based sampling algorithms
based on our theoretical results. In Section 6, we rigorously benchmark our approach against existing
algorithms across several different tasks (e.g., sampling from a target probability distribution, training
a mean-field neural network). Finally, in Section 7, we provide some concluding remarks, including
possible directions for future work.
2. Preliminaries
In this section, we recall some basic concepts relating to optimization on the Wasserstein space.
These include the definitions of the Wasserstein metric, geodesic convexity, and subdifferential
calculus on the Wasserstein space. For a more comprehensive coverage of these topics, we refer to
the excellent books of Ambrosio et al. (2008) and Villani (2008).

2.2. The Wasserstein Space. Given a probability measure µ ∈ P2 (Rd ) and a measurable function

2.4. Subdifferential Calculus in the Wasserstein Space. We are now ready to present
the differential structure of the Wasserstein space. Throughout, we will consider a functional
F : P2 (Rd ) → (−∞, ∞] with domain D(F) = {µ ∈ P2,ac (Rd ) : F(µ) < ∞}.2 Moreover, we will
assume that the functional F is proper and lower semicontinuous. That is, D(F) ̸= ∅, and
F(µ̄) ≤ lim inf µ→µ̄ F(µ) for all µ̄ ∈ P2 (Rd ).
Let µ ∈ D(F). We say that a map ξ ∈ L2 (µ) belongs to the Fréchet subdifferential of F at µ,
and write ξ ∈ ∂F(µ) if

and geodesically convex, since the function H(s) = s log s satisfies all of the necessary conditions

V(µ) + W(µ) + H(µ) is proper, lower semicontinuous, and geodesically convex (Ambrosio et al.,
2008, Theorem 9.4.11).

µ ∈ P2,ac (Rd ), noting that H(s) = s log s satisfies all of the conditions required to compute the W2
gradient (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008, Section 10.4.3). It follows that, for all µ ∈ P2,ac (Rd ), we have

3.2. Gradient Flows. We now turn our attention to how to solve the optimization problem in
(28). A natural solution is to consider a gradient flow of the objective functional over the space of
probability measures.
3.2.1. Wasserstein Gradient Flow. We will focus primarily on the Wasserstein gradient flow
(WGF) of F, defined as the weak solution µ : [0, ∞) → P2 (Rd ) of the continuity equation (Ambrosio
et al., 2008, Chapter 11)
∂µt
+ ∇ · (vt µt ) = 0 , vt ∈ −∂F (µt ).
∂t
Under mild conditions, this equation admits a unique solution for any initial condition (e.g., Theorem
11.1.4, Theorem 11.2.1 in Ambrosio et al., 2008; Proposition 4.13 in Santambrogio, 2017). In
particular, this solution is dictated by the unique minimal selection of the subdifferential (e.g.,
Ambrosio and Savaré, 2007, Theorem 5.3). Thus, for almost all t > 0, we have

(41)

∂µt
+ ∇ · (vt µt ) = 0 , vt = −∇W2 F(µt ).
∂t
In addition, the function t 7→ F(µt ) is decreasing and so, under additional constraints (e.g., F is
geodesically convex), limt→∞ F(µt ) = inf µ∈P2 (Rd ) F(µ) (Ambrosio et al., 2008, Chapter 11).
We can also give a Lagrangian description of the WGF (e.g., Section 8 in Ambrosio et al., 2008;
Section 4 in Santambrogio, 2015). In particular, suppose that we define x = (xt )t≥0 as the integral
curve of the vector fields (vt )t≥0 , viz

(42)

(43)

ẋt = vt (xt ) ,

vt = −∇W2 F(µt ),

with initial condition x0 ∼ µ0 . Then, if we let (µt )t≥0 denote the marginal law of (xt )t≥0 , the
continuity equation in (42) precisely describes the evolution of (µt )t≥0 .

3.2.2. Alternative Gradient Flows. There are, in fact, several other gradient flows which one
could use to solve the minimization problem in (28). These commonly arise as a result of a different
choice of metric over the space of probability measures (e.g., Liu, 2017; Garbuno-Inigo et al., 2020a,b;
Duncan et al., 2023; Lu et al., 2023). Below, we survey several popular alternatives to the Wasserstein
metric, and the corresponding gradient flows.
We mention these since, in principle, our adaptive step size schedule(s) could also be used to obtain
tuning-free versions of the algorithms obtained as time-discretizations of these gradient flows. Indeed,
given an appropriate modification of our existing assumptions (see Section 4.1) – e.g., replacing
geodesic convexity w.r.t. the Wasserstein geometry with geodesic convexity w.r.t. the new geometry
– our theoretical analysis would go through largely unchanged. We leave a more detailed treatment
of this to future work.
The Fisher-Rao Metric. The Fisher-Rao metric (e.g., Amari, 2015; Ay et al., 2015), also known
as the Hellinger distance (Hellinger, 1909) or Hellinger-Kakutani distance (Kakutani, 1948),4 is a
metric over the space M+ (Rd ) of positive measures over Rd , defined via
(48)

functional F : P(Rd ) → R ∪ {+∞}, the gradient with respect to the Fisher-Rao metric can be

The Fisher-Rao gradient flow is then defined as the curve µ : [0, ∞) → P(Rd ) of probability measures
which satisfy the reaction equation (e.g., Lu et al., 2023, Section 2)
∂µt
= Λt µt , Λt = −∇FR F(µt ).
∂t
This is a mean-field equation of birth-death type, where the birth-death rate Λt contains a nonlocal
interaction term that allows global movement of mass.
The convergence properties of the Fisher-Rao gradient flow of the KL divergence (e.g., exponential
convergence rate, independent of the target measure) have recently been established by various
authors (Domingo-Enrich and Pooladian, 2023; Lu et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023b). More recently,
Carrillo et al. (2024a) study their convergence properties for a wider-class of energy functionals. See
also Chen et al. (2024b); Maurais and Marzouk (2024) for some applications to sampling problems.
Other authors have considered a composite of the Wasserstein and Fisher-Rao geometries (e.g.,
Kondratyev et al., 2016; Chizat et al., 2018; Liero et al., 2018). In particular, Gallouët and
Monsaingeon (2017); Lu et al. (2019b); Kondratyev and Vorotnikov (2019); Yan et al. (2024); Tan
and Lu (2025) all study gradient flows with respect to the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao metric. Such
gradient flows allow for both mass transportation and mass teleportation.

research, although the convergence theory remains largely underdeveloped. For a non-exhaustive

3.3. Practical Algorithms. In order to obtain practical optimization algorithms, it will of course
be necessary to discretize the continuous-time gradient flow defined in (41) or, indeed, (50), (53), or
(56). This is the subject of Section 3.3.1.
3.3.1. Time Discretization. We will focus on two common time-discretizations of the continuoustime dynamics in (41).
Forward-Flow Discretization. The first of these is the so-called forward-flow discretization (e.g.,
Wibisono, 2018, Section 2.2.2). This is applicable when F : P2 (Rd ) → (−∞, ∞] takes the general
form in (29), but now with H(µ) = Ent(µ) given specifically by the negative entropy; see also (37).
In its most general form, this discretization is defined as follows. Fix µ0 ∈ P2 (Rd ). Then, for t ≥ 0,

Forward Euler Discretization. The second time-discretization we consider is the forward or
explicit Euler discretization. This yields the so-called Wasserstein subgradient descent algorithm
(Guo et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2022), defined as follows. Fix µ0 ∈ P2 (Rd ). Then, for t ≥ 0, update
(62)

µt+1 = (id − ηt ξt )# µt ,

ξt = ∇W2 F(µt ).

Similar to before, we can also give a Lagrangian description of the Wasserstein subgradient descent
algorithm. Suppose we initialize x0 ∼ µ0 , where µ0 ∈ P2 (Rd ) as above. For t ≥ 0, we update
(63)

xt+1 = xt − ηt ξt (xt ),

ξt = ∇W2 F(µt ).

Then, if we write (µt )t∈N0 for the marginal law of (xt )t∈N0 , then the update equation in (62) precisely
describes the evolution of (µt )t∈N0 .
Other Time Discretizations. There are, of course, other possible time-discretizations of the WGF
in (41), though we do not consider these any further in this paper. These include a backward Euler
discretization, which corresponds to the minimizing movement scheme (MMS) (Ambrosio et al.,
2008, Definition 2.0.6) or Jordan-Kinderlehrer-Otto (JKO) algorithm (Jordan et al., 1998); and a
forward-backward discretization, which yields the Wasserstein proximal gradient algorithm (Salim
et al., 2020; Zhu and Chen, 2025).
Example 1 (Sampling from a Target Probability Distribution). Consider again our
running example: F(µ) = KL(µ∥π), with π ∝ e−U . Substituting (40) into (61), the forward-flow
discretization of the WGF of the KL divergence is given by
p
(64)
xt+1 = xt − ηt ∇U (xt ) + 2ηt zt , zt ∼ N (0, Id ).
This is the well-known Langevin Monte Carlo (LMC) or unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA)
(e.g., Dalalyan and Karagulyan, 2019; Durmus et al., 2019). We note that this algorithm can also
be obtained by considering the Euler-Maruyama discretization of the WGF of KL divergence, as
defined by the overdamped Langevin diffusion in (47).
Meanwhile, substituting (40) into (63), the Wasserstein subgradient descent algorithm for the
KL divergence writes as
(65)

xt+1 = xt − ηt [∇U (xt ) + ∇ log µt (xt )] .

This algorithm is sometimes referred to as deterministic Langevin Monte Carlo (e.g., Grumitt
et al., 2022).
3.3.2. Constrained Domains. In certain applications, we may in fact be interested in solving a
constrained version of the optimization problem in (28), viz

where X ⊆ Rd is a closed, convex set and where P2 (X ) is the set of Borel probability measures
supported on X ⊆ Rd with finite second moment. This includes the unconstrained case X = Rd as
an important special case.
In this setting, we can consider projected versions of the previous algorithms, cf. (57) - (58) and
(62), which ensure that the iterates remains in the feasible set. To be precise, after each iteration we
can make use of the Wasserstein projection operator ProjP2 (X ) : P2 (Rd ) → P2 (X ), which defines the
projection onto the set of probability measures with support contained in X , viz
(67)

ProjP2 (X ) (µ) := arg min W2 (µ, µ̄).
µ̄∈P2 (X )

This projection operator is well defined, and coincides with the pushforward of µ under the Euclidean
projection operator (e.g., Lanzetti et al., 2023, Proposition 3.1). That is, for all µ ∈ P2 (Rd ),

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

4. Main Results
In this section, we present our main results. To be specific, we analyze the convergence of
the forward-flow and the forward Euler discretizations of the WGF, as well as their stochastic
counterparts. We first obtain non-asymptotic convergence rates in the case of a constant step size, in
both nonsmooth and smooth settings. We then introduce an adaptive, tuning-free step size schedule
inspired by the approach in Ivgi et al. (2023). Finally, we establish that the resulting algorithms
achieve the optimal rates of convergence up to logarithmic factors.
4.1. Assumptions. Throughout this section, we will impose the following assumptions.
Assumption 1. The functional F : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] is proper and lower semicontinuous. That
is, D(F) = {µ ∈ P2 (X ) : F(µ) < ∞} ̸= ∅ and F (µ̄) ≤ lim inf µ→µ̄ F(µ) for all µ̄ ∈ P2 (X ).
This is a standard technical condition in the literature on WGFs (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008,
Section 11) and, more generally, optimization on Wasserstein spaces (e.g., Lanzetti et al., 2025).
This is a mild assumption, being satisfied by most of the usual functionals that we will encounter
in practice. For example, the squared Wasserstein distance is proper and lower semicontinuous
(Lanzetti et al., 2025, Corollary 2.19); the potential energy V is proper and lower semicontinuous if
the potential functional V is (Ambrosio et al., 2008, Example 9.3.1); the interaction energy W is
proper and lower semicontinuous if the interaction kernel W is lower semicontinuous and satisfies
W (x, x) < ∞ for some x ∈ X (Ambrosio et al., 2008, Example 9.3.4); and the internal energy H
is proper and lower semicontinuous if the function H is a proper, lower semicontinuous, convex
function, which has superlinear growth at infinity which satisfies H(0) = 0 (Ambrosio et al., 2008,
Example 9.3.6).
Assumption 2. The functional F : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] is geodesically convex. That is, for any
µ, ν ∈ P2 (X ), there exists a constant speed geodesic (λµ→ν
)η∈[0,1] between µ and ν such that, for all
η
η ∈ [0, 1],
(87)

F(λµ→ν

η

This is a standard assumption in both the classical (i.e., Euclidean) optimization literature (e.g.,
Bubeck et al., 2015); and the literature on WGFs (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008). In the context of
sampling as an optimization problem, where F(µ) = KL(µ∥π), it is satisfied when the target density
π(x) ∝ e−U (x) is log-concave (see Section 3.1). This is a rather typical assumption in the sampling
literature (e.g., Durmus et al., 2019; Chewi, 2025). This being said, it is worth noting that the
convergence of WGF-related sampling algorithms (e.g., ULA) can be established under somewhat
weaker assumptions such as the log-Sobolev inequality or Poincaré inequality (e.g., Bakry et al.,
2014; Chewi et al., 2024a). We leave to future work the extension of our results to this setting.
Example 1 (Sampling from a Target Probability Distribution). Consider again our
running example: F(µ) = KL(µ∥π), for some target distribution π ∝ e−U . Suppose that U is lower
semicontinuous, convex, and with negative part satisfying a quadratic growth condition. In this case,
both Assumption 1 and Assumption 2 are satisfied (see Section 3.1). That is, the KL divergence
is proper and lower semicontinuous (e.g., Lanzetti et al., 2025, Example 2.29), and geodesically
convex (e.g., Ambrosio et al., 2008, Theorem 9.4.11).
4.1.1. Nonsmooth Functionals. In the nonsmooth setting, we will impose different assumptions
depending on whether we are considering the forward-flow discretization of the WGF or the forward
Euler discretization of the WGF. To be specific, we will require one of the following.

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

23

Assumption 3.a (Forward-Flow). There exists a continuous function g : P2 (X ) → R+ such that
∥ζ̂(µ)(·, b)∥L2 (µ) ≤ g(µ) for almost all b ∈ B, where ζ̂(µ) : Rd × B → Rd is the stochastic gradient
oracle defined in (74).
Assumption 3.b (Forward Euler). There exists a positive constant 0 < G < ∞ such that
ˆ t )(·, bt )∥L2 (µ ) < G for all t ∈ [T ], where ξ(µ)
ˆ
∥ξ(µ
: Rd × B → Rd is the stochastic gradient oracle
t
defined in (79).
Assumption 3.a stipulates that the stochastic (sub)gradients of the potential energy V : P2 (X ) →
(−∞, ∞] and the interaction energy W : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞]. are pointwise bounded. In the case that
the objective is the KL divergence, it reduces to the assumption that the stochastic (sub)gradient of
the potential energy is pointwise bounded. A stronger version of this condition (uniform boundedness)
has previously appeared in the analysis of stochastic (sub)gradient Langevin dynamics (e.g., Durmus
et al. 2019, Assumption A3; Habring et al. 2024, Assumption A).
Assumption 3.b is significantly stronger than Assumption 3.a, requiring that the stochastic
(sub)gradients of the objective functional F : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] are uniformly bounded along the
path of the algorithm iterates. In particular, it requires boundedness of the stochastic (sub)gradient
of the internal energy (e.g., the negative entropy), which can be problematic even in relatively simple
cases (e.g., Xu and Li, 2024). Nonetheless, this is a somewhat standard assumption in existing
analyses of the (stochastic) Wasserstein gradient descent algorithm, and variants thereof (e.g., Korba
et al., 2020, Assumption A3; Guo et al., 2022, Remark 9; Lanzetti et al., 2023, Assumption 3.2).
More generally, we note that the Euclidean analogues of these assumptions are standard in the
literature on (stochastic) subgradient descent (e.g., Zinkevich, 2003; Moulines and Bach, 2011; Shamir
and Zhang, 2013; Garrigos and Gower, 2023; Zamani and Glineur, 2025), including in the analysis of
parameter-free algorithms (e.g., Orabona and Tommasi, 2017; Cutkosky and Orabona, 2018; Jun
and Orabona, 2019; Ivgi et al., 2023).

∥∇Ub ∥L2 (µ) ≤ Gb for all µ ∈ P2 (X ).
For Assumption 3.a, we just require that ∥ζ̂(µ)(·, b)∥L2 (µ) ≤ g(µ) for some continuous function
g : P2 (X ) → R+ , for all b ∈ B. This assumption is clearly satisfied, defining for example g(µ) = G
for all µ ∈ P2 (X ), where G := maxb∈B Gb .

we have ∥ξ(µt )∥L2 (µt ) ≤ ∥∇Ub ∥L2 (µt ) + ∥∇ log µt ∥L2 (µt ) , so it is sufficient to bound each term
separately. Arguing as above, we can bound the first term by G := maxb∈B Gb . Meanwhile, bounding
the second term is equivalent to bounding the Fisher information, since I(µt ) := ∥∇ log µt ∥2L2 (µt ) .
This is a somewhat strong assumption, which can be hard to verify in practice, and may fail to hold
even for relatively simple examples (e.g., Xu and Li, 2024). Nonetheless, similar assumptions have
appeared elsewhere in the sampling literature (e.g., Korba et al., 2020, Assumption A3).

TUNING-FREE SAMPLING VIA OPTIMIZATION ON THE SPACE OF PROBABILITY MEASURES

4.1.2. Smooth Functionals. In the smooth case, we will also require different assumptions for
the forward-flow discretization and the forward Euler discretization. In particular, we impose the
following.
Assumption 4.a (Forward-Flow). The functional E : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] is L-geodesically-smooth.
That is, there exists a constant 0 < L < ∞ such that, for any v ∈ Tµ P2 (X ), it holds that
(90)

HessW2 E(µ)[v, v] ≤ L∥v∥2L2 (µ) .

Assumption 4.b (Forward Euler). The functional F : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] is L-geodesically-smooth.
That is, there exists a constant 0 < L < ∞ such that, for any v ∈ Tµ P2 (X ), it holds that
(91)

HessW2 F(µ)[v, v] ≤ L∥v∥2L2 (µ) .

Smoothness assumptions are common in the optimization literature (e.g., Boyd and Vandenberghe,
2004; Nesterov, 2018; Garrigos and Gower, 2023), including in the analysis of parameter-free
algorithms (e.g., Ivgi et al., 2023; Khaled et al., 2023). In the Euclidean setting, it is well known
that smoothness can improve the non-asymptotic convergence rate of (stochastic) gradient descent
from O( √1T ) (e.g., Nesterov, 2018, Theorem 3.2.2) to O( T1 ) (e.g., Nesterov, 2018, Theorem 2.1.14).
The same is also true for (stochastic) gradient descent on Riemannian manifolds (e.g., Zhang and
Sra, 2016).
In many cases, smoothness assumptions are stated in terms of a Lipschitz condition on the
gradient of the objective function, rather than a bound on the (operator) norm of its Hessian.
In particular, in the Euclidean case, the function f : Rd → (−∞, ∞] is said to be L-smooth if
∥∇f (x) − ∇f (y)∥ ≤ L∥x − y∥ for all x, y ∈ Rd . This is strictly weaker than the assumption that
∥∇2 f (x)∥op ≤ L for all x ∈ Rd , as it also defines a notion of smoothness for functions that are
not twice differentiable. The two conditions are equivalent, however, when the function f is twice
differentiable (e.g., Garrigos and Gower, 2023, Lemma 2.26).
In our setting, one can similarly characterise the smoothness of the objective functional in terms
of its Wasserstein gradients. In particular, the functional F : P2 (Rd ) → (−∞, ∞] is L-smooth iff,
for any µ ∈ P2,ac (Rd ) and ν ∈ P2 (Rd ), it holds that
(92)

∥∇W2 F(ν) ◦ tνµ − ∇W2 F(µ)∥L2 (µ) ≤ L∥tνµ − id∥L2 (µ) ,

where tνµ is the optimal transport map between µ and ν (e.g., Boumal, 2023, Corollary 10.47). Note
that, unlike the Euclidean case, we do not directly compare the gradients ∇W2 F(ν) and ∇W2 F(µ),
since they live in different (tangent) spaces Tν P2 (Rd ) ⊆ L2 (ν) and Tµ P2 (Rd ) ⊆ L2 (µ).
Regarding optimization over the space of probability measures, smoothness assumptions have
appeared in various works (e.g., Durmus et al., 2019 for ULA; Korba et al., 2020 for SVGD; Salim
et al., 2020 for Wasserstein proximal gradient). Typically, however, such assumptions are stated in
terms of, e.g., the potential V : X → (−∞, ∞] or the interaction kernel W : X → (−∞, ∞], rather
than on the potential energy V : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] or the interaction energy W : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞].
We find the latter more convenient for our analysis. It is, however, straightforward to obtain sufficient
conditions which imply smoothness in the sense of our assumptions. For example, if the potential
V : Rd → (−∞, ∞] is L-smooth (in the Euclidean sense), it is straightforward to show that the
potential energy V : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞] is L-smooth (in the Wasserstein sense).
We conclude this commentary with the remark that Assumption 4.a, which is used in the analysis
of the forward-flow discretization of the WGF, is significantly weaker than Assumption 4.b, which
is used in the analysis of the forward Euler discretization. Indeed, the latter additionally requires
smoothness of the internal energy H : P2 (X ) → (−∞, ∞], which is known not to hold in many
important cases, including the (negative) entropy (e.g., Chewi et al., 2024b, Section 6.2.2).

Remark. In the smooth case, the optimal step size and convergence rate for ULA are quantitatively
different from those for (Euclidean) gradient descent. In the Euclidean case, the optimal choice of
step size is η = L1 , which guarantees a convergence rate of O( T1 ) (e.g., Nesterov, 2018, Theorem
2.1.4). Meanwhile, ULA can only attain a rate of O( √1T ). This is due to the additional bias term
Lηd, which arises due to the heat step in the forward-flow discretization of the WGF.

5. Practical Algorithms
In this section, we introduce practical, particle-based sampling algorithms based on the theoretical
results obtained in Section 4. For simplicity, our exposition focuses on the unconstrained setting. We
emphasize, however, that our algorithms can easily be generalized to constrained setting according
to the extension discussed in Section 3.3.2.

discretization of the WGF (see Sections 4.2 - 4.3). Recall, from (57) - (58), the general form

5.1.3. Discussion. We conclude this section with some brief remarks regarding the algorithms
introduced thus far.
Extensions. Naturally, one can also consider stochastic gradient (i.e., mini-batch) variants of these
algorithms, in which gradients are replaced by (unbiased) stochastic estimates (see Section 3.3.3).
Indeed, this is justified by our theoretical analysis in Section 4.2.3. In Example 1, this leads to a
step-size-free version of stochastic-gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD) (Welling and Teh, 2011).
We will refer to this algorithm, summarized in Algorithm 2, as SGLD x Fuse.

Computational Considerations. It is worth noting that ULA x Fuse (and SGLD x Fuse)
differ from their classical formulation (e.g., Neal, 1992; Roberts and Tweedie, 1996; Welling and
Teh, 2011) in the sense that they involve a collection of (interacting) particles, rather than just a
single particle. To be specific, in the classical setting, one would simulate a single trajectory
P (xt )t≥0 ,
and then estimate expectations with respect to the target measure as Ex∼π [f (x)] ≈ T1 Tt=1 f (xt ).
On the other hand,
we simulate multiple trajectories (xit )i=1:n
t≥0 , and approximate expectations as
1 PN
Ex∼π [f (x)] ≈ N i=1 f (xit ). This is sometimes referred to in the literature as parallel-chain ULA or
parallel-chain SGLD (e.g., Futami et al., 2020, 2021).
One can, of course, consider hybrids of these approaches, in which expectations with respect to
the target measure
by averaging over the trajectories of multiple particles, viz,
P are1approximated
PT
i )]. This approach is particularly useful for ULA x Fuse since one
Ex∼π [f (x)] ≈ N1 N
[
f
(x
t
i=1 T
t=1
may require a large number of samples to obtain accurate estimates of expectations with respect to
the target measure, but only a (relatively) small number of particles to obtain a performant estimate
of the Fuse step size schedule (η̂tn )t≥0 . In this situation, one can also leverage parallel computing
environments (e.g., GPUs or arrays of CPUs) for more efficient computation, as independent sets of
trajectories can be computed in parallel.
Limitations. It is worth emphasizing that our theoretical results are only directly applicable to
Example 1. In particular, geodesic convexity does not hold either for the squared KSD (Korba
et al., 2021, Section 4), or the objective functionals encountered in the training of mean-field neural
networks (e.g., Lascu et al., 2024, Example 3.3). Nonetheless, our adaptive step size schedule is still
applicable in these cases, with strong empirical performance (see Section 6).
5.2. The Forward Euler Discretization. We now turn our attention to the forward Euler
discretization of the WGF (see Appendices A.1 - A.2). In the interest of space, and given its
relevance to the sampling problem, we will now focus only on the case where the internal energy in
the objective functional coincides with the (negative) entropy: F(µ) = E(µ) + Ent(µ). In this case,
the forward Euler discretization of the WGF is defined as follows, cf. (63). Let x0 ∼ µ0 , for some
µ0 ∈ P2 (Rd ). Then, for t ≥ 0, writing µt = Law(xt ), update
xt+1 = xt − ηt (∇W2 E(µt )(xt ) + ∇ log µt (xt ))

5.2.1. Particle-Based Approximation. In general, we cannot implement these dynamics directly,
since both (µt )t≥0 and (∇ log µt )t≥0 , are unknown. We would like, as before, to approximate the
dynamics using a system of interacting particles. In this case, however, we have the additional
difficulty that ∇ log µ is only well defined when µ ∈ P2,ac (Rd ). In particular, it is not well defined
when µ ∈ P2 (Rd ) \ P2,ac (Rd ) is a discrete measure.
Kernel-Based Approximation. To circumvent this, we will use a kernel-based approximation,
following the approach popularised in Liu and Wang (2016), and recently extended to the meanfield setting by Chazal et al. (2025). In particular, we will replace the Wasserstein gradient
∇W2 F(µ) := [∇W2 E(µ) + ∇ log µ] in (196) - (197) by its kernel smoothed approximation, viz

, as in (207). This difference arises due to the fact that the original update equation was derived by substituting an
n
empirical measure into the objective functional itself,P
rather than its Wasserstein gradient. This defines an objective
n
i
j
1
function F : (Rd )n → R, given by F ([xi ]n
i=1 ) = 2N 2
i,j=1 kπ (x , x ), over the finite-dimensional Euclidean space
d n
(R ) . This objective function can be optimized directly via (Euclidean) gradient descent, which yields the original
update equation. We refer to Korba et al. (2021, Section 3.2) for further details.

6. Numerical Results
In this section, we benchmark the performance of Fuse across several experiments involving both
synthetic and real data. We consider examples which satisfy the assumptions of the previous sections,
as well as examples which violate one or more of these assumptions (e.g., geodesic convexity). We
performed all experiments using a MacBook Pro 16" (2021) laptop with Apple M1 Pro chip and
16GB of RAM.
6.1. Example 1: Sampling via KL Divergence Minimization. We first consider the task of
minimizing F (µ) = KL(µ∥π), for some target probability distribution π ∝ e−U .
6.1.1. Gaussian Target. We first consider an example where the target π is given by a (unimodal)
Gaussian distribution, namely,
 1

(210)
π(x) = N (x|mπ , Σπ ) ∝ exp − (x − mπ )⊤ Σ−1
π (x − mπ ) .
2
In this case, it is possible to generate true samples from the target, and thus compute a number of
different performance metrics. This target is also (strongly) log-concave, meaning the KL divergence
is (strongly) geodesically convex, and our assumptions are satisfied.
Baseline Comparison. In Figure 1, we compare the performance of ULA and ULA x Fuse for
a 10-dimensional Gaussian target distribution π = N (mπ , Σπ ), with Σπ = diag(σπ2 ). We measure
performance using a particle-based approximation of the KL divergence.7 We report results averaged
over 10 random seeds: for each experiment, we draw mπ uniformly at random in [−2, 2], and σπ2
uniformly at random in [1, 5]. In each case, we initialize using a standard Gaussian: µ0 = N (0, Id ).
We run ULA for a grid of uniformly logarithmically spaced step sizes η ∈ [10−6 , 100 ], and ULA x
Fuse for the same grid of values of rε . For ULA x Fuse, we report results for both the practical,
particle-based step size schedule (orange) and also the ideal, mean-field step size schedule (green).8
The empirical results closely reflect our theoretical findings. In particular, the performance of
ULA x Fuse essentially matches the performance of optimally tuned constant step size ULA. Our
results also illustrate the robustness of ULA x Fuse to the choice of the initial movement parameter
rε . Indeed, for any choice of rε within the reported range of [10−6 , 100 ], the performance of ULA x
Fuse is essentially identical.
It is also noteworthy that the ideal, mean-field ULA x Fuse step size schedule (green) slightly
outperforms the practical, particle-based ULA x Fuse step size schedule (orange). This should not
be surprising: recall that the ideal ULA x Fuse step size was originally derived as a proxy for an
optimal fixed ULA step size, as measured by an upper bound for KL(µ̄T ∥π) (see Section 4.2.2). In a
similar way, the practical, particle-based ULA x Fuse step size can be viewed as a proxy for an
optimal ULA fixed step size, but now as measured by a looser upper bound for KL(µ̄T ∥π), in which
the W2 distance is replaced by the corresponding L2 distance. In principle, one could replicate all
of the results in Sections 4.2 - 4.3 for this step size, but now with any W2 distance replaced by
the corresponding L2 distance. Thus, in particular, this step size schedule attains the same O( √1T )
convergence rate as the ideal step size schedule (in terms of the iteration number), but now with a
slightly worse dependence on the problem diameter.
7Our approximation is computed as follows. First, we compute the empirical mean m̂n and covariance Σ̂n of the

particles (xit )n
i=1 . We then approximate µt ≈ µ̄t , where µ̄t = N (m̂t , Σ̂t ) is a Gaussian distribution with the mean
and covariance just computed. This is justified by the fact that, if µ0 = N (m0 , Σ0 ) and π = N (mπ , Σπ ) are both
Gaussian, then µt remains Gaussian for all t ≥ 0 (see above). Finally, we approximate KL(µt ∥π) ≈ KL(µ̄n
t ∥π), which
we can compute using the standard formula for the KL divergence between two Gaussians (e.g., Pardo, 2006).
8Under the assumption that the initial distribution µ = N (m , Σ ) is also Gaussian, the WGF (µ )

for all t ≥ 0 (e.g., Wibisono, 2018). It is thus possible to compute the theoretically optimal step size schedule, cf.,
(174), exactly, since all quantities are available in closed form (see Section 4.2.2). In particular, we do not have to
resort to a particle approximation, cf. (177), or a coarse upper bound for the Wasserstein distance, cf. (178).

Particle Approximation. In Figure 2, we further investigate the behavior of the ideal, mean-field
ULA x Fuse step size schedule (ηt )t≥0 , as well as the practical, particle-based step size schedule
(η̂tn )t≥0 , for several different values of the initial movement parameter rε . For comparison, we also
plot the optimal fixed ULA step size, with optimality here determined (retrospectively) as the step
size for which ULA achieves the lowest value of KL(µT ∥π) after T = 500 iterations.
We make several observations. First, regardless of the value of rε , the ULA x Fuse step sizes
converge (from above) to the fixed optimal step size. This should not be surprising: the ULA x
Fuse step size is an upper bound for the theoretically optimal fixed step size, as discussed in Section
4.2.2. Second, after an initial decrease, the ULA x Fuse step size decreases as a function of the
iteration number. This is encouraging: as indicated by the theory, the larger the iteration budget,
the smaller one should take the step size. Finally, the practical, particle-based step size schedule
(uniformly) upper bounds the ideal, mean-field step size schedule. Once again, this is to be expected:
the approximate step size schedule relies on substituting an upper bound for the Wasserstein distance
in the numerator of the ideal step size schedule, cf. Section 5. Interestingly, the disparity between
the two step size schedules is significantly diminished for larger values of rε , since in this case rε
dominates the numerator (particularly for smaller values of t).

Figure 3. A comparison of the ULA and ULA x Fuse for different distances between
the initial distribution and the 10-dimensional Gaussian target distribution. We plot

Robustness to the Initial Distance. In Figure 3, we continue to benchmark the performance of
ULA x Fuse. In particular, we compare the performance of ULA and ULA x Fuse as we vary the
distance from the initial distribution µ0 = N (0, Id ) to the target distribution π = N (mπ , Id ), for
mπ ∈ [0.2, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0]. This figure illustrates the robustness of ULA x Fuse to the initial distance,
a hyper-parameter which can have a significant impact on the optimal (range of) fixed ULA step
size(s). We should emphasize that this effect is particularly acute for smaller iteration budgets, and
may diminish as the iteration budget increases.
Robustness to Anisotropy. In Figure 4, we compare the performance of ULA and ULA x Fuse
for Gaussian targets with increasing levels of anisotropy, i.e., increasing values of the condition
number κ = λλmax
, where λmax and λmin denote the maximum and minimum eigenvalues of the
min
covariance Σπ of the target distribution. As this figure makes evident, the optimal fixed ULA step
size is highly dependent on the condition number. Meanwhile, ULA x Fuse is robust to the level
of anisotropy. In all cases, it essentially matches the performance of an optimally tuned ULA, for
any reasonable value of the initial movement parameter rε . Interestingly, these results also indicate
that ULA is increasingly sensitive to the choice of step size as the anisotropy increases. That is,
the range of step sizes for which ULA achieves near-optimal performance tightens as the anisotropy
increases (compare, e.g., the “U-shape” of the left-hand panel in Figure 4 to the “V-shape” of the
right-hand panel). This provides further motivation for the use of an automated step size scheduler
like ULA x Fuse, which is agnostic to the level of anisotropy.
Remark. Another effective method for dealing with anisotropic target distributions is to use a
preconditioned version of the overdamped Langevin dynamics (e.g., Garbuno-Inigo et al., 2020b;
Carrillo and Vaes, 2021; Burger et al., 2024). For a particular choice of preconditioning, i.e., the
covariance of the current distribution, the resulting dynamical system corresponds to the gradient flow
of the KL divergence with respect to the so-called Kalman-Wasserstein metric (see Section 3.2.2).
This contrasts with the standard overdamped Langevin dynamics, which represents the gradient flow
of the KL divergence with respect to the Wasserstein metric (Jordan et al., 1998). After space-time
discretization, the preconditioned variant yields an algorithm known as affine invariant Langevin
dynamics (ALDI) Garbuno-Inigo et al. (2020b). In principle, one could use a version of our step size
schedule within this algorithm, with Wasserstein gradients replaced by Kalman-Wasserstein gradients.
Moreover, replacing our existing assumptions (see Section 4.1) with the appropriate analogues (e.g.,
geodesic convexity in the Kalman-Wasserstein space), one could replicate our earlier theoretical results
(see Section 4).

6.1.2. Bayesian Logistic Regression. We next consider a Bayesian logistic regression problem
N
(e.g., Held and Holmes, 2006). Consider i.i.d. observations D = (xi , yi )N

Gaussian Prior, Synthetic Data. In our first example, we consider a Gaussian prior for the
regression coefficients β = (β0 , β1 , . . . , βp )⊤ , where β0 denotes the intercept. In particular, we assume
that βj ∼ N (0, λ−1 ) for j = 1, . . . , p, fixing λ = 5.0 in our experiments. Meanwhile, the intercept is
left unpenalized; formally, β0 ∼ N (0, λ−1
0 ), with λ0 = 0.
We generate synthetic data as follows. We generate a “true” regression coefficient β = (β0 , . . . , βp )⊤ ,
v
setting β0 = 0 and βj = s ||v||j 2 for j = 2, . . . , p, where vj ∼ N (0, 1) and s > 0 is a parameter which
determines the signal strength. This normalization ensures that ||β||2 = s. We generate feature
vectors according to xi ∼ N (0, Σρ ) for i = 1, . . . , N , where (Σρ )jk = ρ|j−k| , and ρ ≥ 0 controls the
correlation. We compute scaled logits zi = τ −1 β ⊤ xi , where τ > 0 is a temperature parameter which
controls class separability, with class probabilities given by pi = ezi /(1 + ezi ). We then sample
observations yi ∼ Bernoulli(pi ), for i = 1, . . . , N . Finally, to introduce mild stochasticity, we
independently flip each label with (small) probability πflip . In our experiments, we set d = 8 and
N = 500. We set the other hyperparameters as s = 3.0, ρ = 0.2, τ = 0.6, πflip = 0.01. The resulting
model is non-separable but moderately well-conditioned, with a controlled signal-to-noise ratio
determined s = 3.0 and τ = 0.6, and a weak correlation structure ρ = 0.2 between features.
We run ULA and ULA x Fuse using n = 100 particles and for T = 500 iterations. The step size
η (ULA) and the initial movement parameter rε (ULA x Fuse) are varied over a logarithmic grid
[10−5 , 10−1 ]. We repeat all experiments over 10 random seeds. In order to assess the performance of
each sampler, we approximate the posterior mean of four functionals fi : Rd → R, using the particles
output at the final iteration. In particular, we consider the functionals
p(yi | xi , β) = pyi i (1 − pi )1−yi ,

(212)

f1 : β 7→ β0 ,

f2 : β → β 1 ,

f3 : β 7→ ||β||1 ,

f4 : β → ||β||22 .

We also compute empirical 95% credible intervals for each of the regression coefficients. We compare
these values to “ground-truth” values, which we obtain via a long-run (T = 106 ) ULA reference chain
with small fixed step size (η = 10−4 ).
Our results are displayed in Figure 6 and Figure 7. In Figure 6 we compare the posterior mean
estimates obtained via ULA and ULA x Fuse, with posterior mean estimates obtained via a long-run
ULA chain, which we treat as a proxy for the ground truth. In each box plot, we summarize the
distribution of posterior mean estimates obtained over 10 independent seeds, corresponding to 10
distinct synthetic datasets. For the purpose of illustration, we report results for ULA using two
sub-optimal choices of the fixed step size: η = 10−5 and η = 10−1 . To ensure a fair comparison, we
report results for ULA x Fuse using the same values of the initial movement parameter: rε = 10−5
and rε = 10−1 . Across all of the considered functionals, ULA x Fuse produces posterior mean
estimates that are substantially closer to the ground truth. While standard ULA can perform well
in this example, given a well-tuned step size, its performance significantly deteriorates when the
step size is either overly conservative (top) or overly aggressive (bottom). In contrast, ULA x Fuse
achieves comparable performance across a wide range of rε values, indicating that there is no need
to manually tune this parameter.
These observations are further supported by the results in Figure 7, in which we plot the empirical
95% credible intervals obtained using ULA and ULA x Fuse. In this case, rather than considering
a single fixed step size η (or initial movement parameter r0 ), we aggregate results across multiple
logarithmically spaced step sizes (or initial movement parameters) in the range [10−5 , 10−1 ]. The
result is the same: across all coefficients, the ULA x Fuse intervals much more closely resemble
those obtained using the true posterior.

Prediction accuracy

Prediction accuracy

Hierarchical Prior, Real Data. We next consider a hierarchical specification for the prior over
the regression coefficients β = (β1 , . . . , βd )⊤ , following MacKay (1995); Gershman et al. (2012); Liu
and Wang (2016). In particular, we assume that p(β|α) = N (β|0, α−1 Id ), p(α) = Γ(α|a, b), where
a, b ∈ R+ are hyper-parameters. We will assume throughout that a = 1, b = 0.01. We are then
interested in sampling from the posterior distribution π(θ) := p(θ|D), where θ = (α, β)⊤ . We now
test our algorithms on the Covertype dataset (Blackard, 1998), which consists of 581,012 data points
and 54 features. Due to the size of the dataset, it is necessary to use stochastic gradients, which we
compute using mini-batches of size 100. To evaluate the performance of each algorithm, we randomly
partition the data into a training dataset (80%) and a testing dataset (20%). For each method, we
use n = 20 particles, T = 1000 iterations, and report results averaged over 10 random runs.
The results for SGLD x Fuse and SVGD x Fuse are shown in Figures 8 and 9, respectively. In
particular, we plot the predictive (test) accuracy as a function of the step size (left-hand panel)
and the iteration number (right-hand panel). The results are similar to before. In particular, the
predictive performance of SGLD x Fuse and SVGD x Fuse is highly robust to the choice of the
initial movement parameter rε , and essentially matches the optimal predictive performance of SGLD
and SVGD with a fixed step size.

Figure 10. A comparison of SGLD and SGLD x Fuse for different batch sizes, for
Bayesian logistic regression on a subset of the Covertype dataset. We plot the predictive
accuracy as a function of the step size η (SGLD) or the initial movement parameter rε (SGLD x
Fuse) after T = 1000 iterations. We use mini-batch gradients with mini-batch sizes [10, 100, 1000].
We continue to investigate the performance of SGLD x Fuse in Figure 10, where we perform a
sensitivity analysis with respect to the mini-batch size used to compute the stochastic gradients. To
reduce the computational burden, we consider a randomly selected subset of the Covertype dataset
with 20, 000 data points. We then run each algorithm using n = 20 particles, T = 1000 iterations,
and mini-batch sizes [10, 100, 1000]. Across all mini-batch sizes, SGLD exhibits the characteristic
U-shaped performance profile: small step sizes lead to slow exploration and under-dispersed samples,
while large step sizes cause numerical instability and divergence of the chain. In contrast, SGLD x
Fuse maintains near-optimal predictive accuracy across several orders of magnitude in the initial
movement parameter.
Comparing the three panels in Figure 10, it is clear that the mini-batch size has a relatively
pronounced effect on the performance of SGLD (Dubey et al., 2016; Baker et al., 2019; Putcha
et al., 2023). For small mini-batches (left-hand panel), the stochastic gradient noise dominates,
and the performance of SGLD degrades sharply except in a narrow range of step sizes. SGLD x
Fuse, however, maintains stable accuracy despite this increased gradient variance, suggesting that
the adaptive update balances the stochasticity by automatically reducing the effective step size
when gradients become noisy. As the mini-batch size increases (center panel, right-hand panel),
both methods improve in stability, but SGLD x Fuse consistently matches or exceeds the best
performance of SGLD, with no need for tuning. Notably, for the largest batch size, SGLD x Fuse
achieves near-optimal accuracy across the full range of tested step sizes, whereas SGLD still requires
careful step size selection. More broadly, our results indicate that the optimal fixed step size for
SGLD is dependent on the mini-batch size. SGLD x Fuse removes the need to (re)-tune this
parameter across different mini-batch configurations.

squared error loss ℓ(y, y ′ ) = (y − y ′ )2 , ignore the regularizer so that r(x) = 0, and let hx (z) =

in the same way as the training data. We report results averaged over 10 random seeds.
The results, displayed in Figures 11 - 12, are consistent with those observed in the previous
experiments. MFLD x Fuse and VGD x Fuse maintain a consistently low test error across a broad
range of step sizes, while the performance of MFLD and VGD deteriorates rapidly as the step size
increases or decreases outside a narrow range. In particular, if the step size is chosen too small,
MFLD and VGD do not converge within the given number of iterations, and if the step size is chosen
too large, the bias incurred is substantial. These results highlight that our proposed tuning-free
framework generalizes to models where our assumptions do not necessarily hold (e.g., non-geodesically
convex), maintaining their efficacy in complex targets with pronounced multimodality.
